RAJYA SABHA 
Tuesday, the 5th December, 2023 / 14 Agrahayana, 1945 (Saka) 
The House met at eleven of the clock, 
MR. CHAIRMAN in the Chair.  
BIRTHDAY GREETINGS 
MR. CHAIRMAN : Birthday Greetings and she walks in at the right time. Hon. 
Members, I am pleased to extend birthday greetings to hon. Member of Parliament, 
Shrimati Rajani Ashokrao Patil.  Shrimati Patil is a second term Member of this august 
House, from 2013 to 2019 and from 2021 till  date and further; she had also been a 
Member of Lok Sabha from 1 996 to 1998. From that perspective, she belongs to the 
category of axed MPs as I belong, who ne ver completed their term. Shrimati Patil 
belongs to a renowned family of freedom fi ghters. She is granddaughter of late Shri 
Vishnu Ganesh Pingle, a founding member of Ghadar Movement and her father was 
also a veteran freedom fighter and a member of Central Legislative Assembly in 1937. 
Her mother was also a freedom fi ghter.  Shrimati Rajani Patil is fond of music, theatre 
and travelling. She is married  to Shri Ashok Patil and ha s two sons, Aditya and Amit 
and a daughter, Tejaswini Patil.  
Hon. Members, on my own and your behalf, I wish her a long, healthy and 
happy life and to be ever blessed in public life.  
Now, papers to be laid on the Table.

MESSAGE FROM LOK SABHA 
 
The Advocates (Amendment) Bill, 2023 
 
SECRETARY-GENERAL:  Sir, with your kind permission, I rise to report that the Lok 
Sabha, at its sitting held on 4 th December, 2023, has agreed  without any amendment 
to the Advocates (Amendment) Bill, 2023, which was passed by the Rajya Sabha at 
its sitting held on the 3
rd August, 2023.

Delay in disbursement of funds dues to MGNREGA scheme in West Bengal 
 
SHRI SAMIRUL ISLAM (West Bengal) :  Respected Chairman, Sir, the MGNREGA is 
not just a scheme of the Union Government.  It was an Act esta blished in 2005. The 
10
[RAJYA SABHA]

 
primary focus of this Act is improvement of rural livelihoods.  Over the last 20 years, 
the scheme has become the lifeline for margin alised people in India, with crores of 
citizens depending on it as this is their right.  The programme contributes to rural 
infrastructure and assets, indirectly addr essing the basic demands and employment 
needs of a large section of people in rura l India.  However, my concern today is 
MGNREGA and the neglect faced by the poor  people of West Bengal.  The Budget 
allocation for MGNREGA has been reduced to Rs. 60,000 crore in the current fiscal 
year, 2023-24.  West Bengal stands out as the only instance where the Union Ministry 
for Rural Development has completely wit hheld its allocation since 2021.  The total 
due towards wages for lakhs of job-card ho lders alone is around Rs. 3,500 crore, 
with a total pending due worth Rs. 7,000 crore. These figures highlight the situation 
where the Union Government has not sanction ed even a penny for the poor people of 
West Bengal under the scheme named after the Father of the Na tion in the current 
fiscal year. Consequently, over 20 lakh pe ople, dependent on the rural scheme, also 
known as the 100-day-work scheme, are st ruggling to access their right to income. 
Their plight sends an adverse message to society and is breaking the backbone of the 
rural economy in Bengal. This matter should be a subject of debate in this House, 
questioning who has the authority to depriv e the poor people of Eastern State of 
Bengal. Sir, I want to remind you that West Bengal is the State that stood first multiple 
times in implementation of MGNREGA.  
Sir, another significant point is that under MGNREGA, the Government should 
pay wages to labourers within fifteen days after the completion of works. ...(Time Bell 
rings.)... Ironically, labourers from Bengal have not received their dues even after two 
years. This raises concerns  about the adherence to the rules and the impact on the 
lives of those who are dependent on this vital scheme. Thank you, Sir.

Rising concentration of wealth in the hands of few people in India 
 
DR. JOHN BRITTAS (Kerala) : Sir, we are presiding over mu ltiple levels of paradox. 
On the one count, the Constitution talks about exhorting the Governments to bring 
policy to reduce the inequalities. On the ot her count, the policies of the Government 
are instrumental in surging inequalities.  
12
[RAJYA SABHA]

 
Sir, the OXFAM India Report, 'Survival of the richest' talked about one per cent 
of India owning more than 40 per cent of the total wealth, while, the bottom 50 per 
cent share only 3 per cent. We had a strange phenomenon during the Covid times, 84 
per cent of the people lost their incomes, or reduced their incomes drastically 
whereas the corporate increased their prof its. The Government brought down the 
Corporate Tax and thereby enabling the corporate to benefit at least Rs. 2 lakh crores 
per year. The OXFAM Report adds that wealth of billionaires surged at an 
unprecedented rate of Rs. 2.5 crore per minute. Yesterday only, the Finance Minister, 
while replying in Lok Sabha,stated that more  than Rs. 10 lakh crores of loans of the 
industrialists have been written off. 2300 people have been castigated as wilful 
defaulters eating up more than  Rs. 2 lakh crores. Sir, we all harp on India being the 
fifth biggest economy; statistics and figures can be fake unless we analyse them.  If 
you just look at the real picture; we are 127
th on the ladder, and in per capita GDP, we 
are behind Sri Lanka and ev en Bangladesh. On one count, we have increased the 
numbers, the number of billionaires in the country have gone up and we are third in 
the world in the count of billionaires. We have almost 169 billionaires in India.

breathing for sustenance; they were demanding for land reforms; they were

rally because their friends have benefited and their closest friend benefited. The 
market cap went more than Rs. 12 lakh crores. So, this is the paradox which our 
country is presiding.  The other day, after the election victory, you must have heard

Increasing Loan App crimes and other fraudulent activities in the country 
 
SHRI A.A. RAHIM (Kerala): Sir, I am in front of this au gust House on behalf of the 
victims and people who lost their valuable lives due to loan app crimes.  Recently, 
there has been a significant surge in loan app crimes in the country.  These crimes 
involve fraudulent activities committed by using digital platforms primarily through 
mobile applications offering quick and easy loans.  This is a matter of grave concern 
and requires urgent action.  Sir, how could loan apps exploit a large number of 
people in our country? The reasons are increasing unemployment and living 
expenditure.  Common people, especially th e youth, are running from bank to bank 
for ordinary loans, but our nationalized bank s are in a competition to provide loans to 
corporates without granting any loans to the needy masses.  Our conventional 
banking system is denying loans to these p oor and needy.  The situation will naturally 
lead the common man to depend on other so urces of money.  Sir, the main reasons 
for the increase in the loan apps is the flawed policies of the Government.  The 
situation of people who borrow money through loan apps is very pathetic.  Once the 
payment is left or if they fail to pay th e amount demanded by the app owners, the 
customer is blackmailed and such mafi as commit heinous crimes, including 
circulating deep-fake videos and morphed images by usin g data extracted from the 
clients during the registration process.  This leads to clients' suicides and other 
problems. How the Union Government deals with it is that their only response is 
'silence'. I urge the Government to expand  more credit schemes to nationalized 
banks.  Strengthening the cooperative sect or and promoting credit schemes through

arena. For this, awareness should be created among the public about which loans are 
legitimate and those which are not should be published at regular intervals.

 so worse that people of Tamil 
Nadu are put to very big hardship.

not able to push water because of sea water gushing into rivers.  All water bodies are 
overflowing. Many tanks have been breached. Roads have been very badly damaged 
and eroded everywhere due to running water.  However best the infrastructure of the 
city may be, the fury of natu re is so hard and worse, no thing can withstand that. So, 
all roads have been eroded.  The essential needs of the people could not be supplied; 
though milk vendors and medical facilities can move, but roads are not accessible.

Minister, and the whole Cabinet are on their heels.  The officials of the corporation, 
workers, doctors, sanitation workers, fire service, rescue teams, electricity board and 
everyone have been working on a war-footing.

lives of people. Electricity was suspende d as a precautionary and temporary measure 
which saved hundreds of people who, otherw ise, would have met with electrocution.

the State Government in its efforts to safeguard the people of Tamil Nadu.

SHRI VAIKO (Tamil Nadu) : Mr. Deputy Chairman, Sir, thank you very much for the 
opportunity given to me. The Sri Lankan Navy is continuously attacking our Tamil

brutally attacked and their boats and fishing trawlers were damaged. Their fishing 
nets were destroyed in the past.  Recently,  they have started capturing their boats 
22
[RAJYA SABHA]

 
and put them into auction.  Unfortunately, it has become a serial story.  There have 
been several incidents of Sri Lankan navy pers onnel even firing at Indian fishermen in 
the Palk Strait and seizing th eir boats.  Recently, 22 fishermen were arrested by the 
Sri Lankan Navy.  The frequent arrests and seizure of boats have shattered the 
livelihood of many fishermen.   
 I would urge upon the Union Governme nt to take urgent steps to secure 
release of all the fishermen and their boats unconditionally.  The Government should 
also put diplomatic pressure on the Sri La nkan Government for giving compensation 
under Solatium Scheme to the next of kin who were shot dead and whose boats were 
damaged by the Sri Lankan Navy. They shou ld hold a high-level talk to find a 
permanent solution to this non-stop hara ssment of our fishermen by the Sri Lankan 
Navy. Aren’t Tamil Nadu fishermen the citizens of  India?  If they are citizens of India,  
what is our Navy doing there?  It is sitting like a lame duck in the Bay of Bengal.

Need to address the issue of missing women in India 
 
DR. FAUZIA KHAN (Maharashtra):   Thank you, Sir. The issue of missing women in 
the country is alarming with 10,61,648 women missing alone in the years 18 and above 
and 2,50,430 girls below 18 years have repo rted missing between the years 2019-21.  
Over two lakh women went missing in th e State of Madhya Pradesh alone between 
the years 2019-21.  Yet the reasons have re mained undisclosed.  Sir, the suspected 
reasons behind the disappearance of these wo men are diverse like trafficking, trade 
and, in minuscule cases, elopement.  Tacklin g this issue requires collective action on 
various fronts, strengthening law enforcem
ent to identify networks, enhancing border 
control, establishing a database for accura te identification of the reasons for the 
disappearances. Additionally, the numbers have come as a surprise to many 
indicating the lack of awareness of the incidents of disappearance of women.  
Therefore, public awareness campaigns,  community involvement and educational 
38
[RAJYA SABHA]

 
programmes can help prevent vulnerable indivi duals from falling prey  to traffickers.  
Despite designated stop centers, the unp recedented increase in  numbers suggests 
the possibility of organized criminal activ ity. A recommendation to address the issue 
of missing women involves the establishm ent and maintenance of a comprehensive 
register by the Government documenting details of every woman reported missing. 
This register should serve as a centralized  database enabling efficiency tracking and 
tracing efforts.  Sir, additionally, specializ ed teams should be formed to investigate 
each case with the focus on identifying potential foul play. These teams could 
comprise experts from law enforcement, so cial services and legal professionals to 
ensure a thorough and coordinated appro ach in understandin g the circumstances 
behind each disappearance to ensure accountability and effectiveness.

MR. CHAIRMAN: Q.No. 16. Shri Sanjay Raut. 
 
Charging customer for insurance premium 
 
*16. SHRI SANJAY RAUT: Will the Minister of FINANCE be pleased to state: 
 
(a) whether it is a fact that several Ba nks across the country have been charging 
customers for the Union Government’s  multiple insurance and pension 
schemes without their consent; 
(b) if so, the details thereof and Government's response thereto; 
(c) the amount of insurance premium co llected by the banks for the accidental 
insurance coverage since last three years indicating the amount spent by 
Government as a social security to the account holders; and 
(d) the amount disbursed to the families as  accidental insurance claim during last 
three years?  
 
THE MINISTER OF STATE IN THE MI NISTRY OF FINANCE (DR. BHAGWAT 
KARAD) : (a) to (d) A Statement is laid on the Table of the House. 
 
Statement 
 
(a) and (b) The Union Government’s mult iple insurance and pension schemes are 
consent driven and premium is debited after mandate is given by customer. 
 
(c) and (d) Pradhan Mantri Suraksha Bima Yojana (PMSBY) provides an affordable 
accidental insurance cover for the poor and und erprivileged sections of the society. It 
is the endeavor of the Government to prov ide low cost accidental insurance of Rs 2 
lakh to all individuals in the age group of 18-70 years at a very low premium amount of 
Rs 20 per annum.  
The Premium amount is collected by ba nks on behalf of insurance companies 
and the entire premium amount is born e by the customer. Details of premium 
collected and accidental insurance claim am ount disbursed for PMSBY during last

Achievements under AYURGYAN scheme 
 
*17. Shri Pramod Tiwari: Will the Minister of AYUSH be pleased to state:  
 
(a) whether there are recent achieveme nts of Government under AYURGYAN 
scheme and if so, the details thereof; 
(b) the measures being taken to provide su
pport for capacity building, training and 
continuing medical education in AYUSH; 
(c) the number of AYUSH teachers, doct ors, paramedical and other personnel 
being trained under AYURGYAN scheme; and 
(d) the details thereof, year-wise and State-wise? 
 
THE MINISTER OF STATE IN THE MINISTRY OF AYUSH (DR. MUNJAPARA 
MAHENDRABHAI): (a) to (d) A Statement is laid on the table of the House. 
 
 
 
46
[RAJYA SABHA]

 
Statement  
 
(a) and (b) Yes, Central Sector Scheme of Ayurgyan under Ministry of Ayush has 
two components viz. i) Capacity Building & Continuing Medical Education in Ayush; 
and ii) Research & Innovation in Ayush.  The scheme is effective from FY 2021-22.  
The achievements under Ayurgyan Scheme since its inception are as below:- 
 
i) Under Capacity Building and CME in Ayush component
: 
 Fund to the tune of Rs.2.70 Crore during  FY 2021-22, Rs.6.25 Crore during FY 
2022-23 and Rs.2.50 Crores during FY  2023-24 (as on 01/12/2023) was 
released/ disbursed to 82 eligible instit utes/organizations across the country to 
conduct 131 training program in different  subjects for Ayush teachers, doctors, 
paramedics and Ayush/Non-Ayush Scientist/Doctors; 
 3330 number of trainees have been trained so far. 
 Under Capacity Building & CME in Ay ush component of Ayurgyan Scheme, 
financial assistance is being provided to the eligible organizations for holding CME 
program for Ayush personnel viz. teachers, doctors, paramedics and other 
personnel.   
 
ii) Research & Innovation in Ayush component
: 
  Fund to the tune of Rs.1.76 Crore duri ng FY 2021-22, Rs.2.82 Crore during FY 
2022-23 and Rs.1.47 Crore during FY 2023-24 (as on 01/12/2023) was released 
for 21, 25 & 18 Research Projects respectively.    
 Detailed guidelines of Ayurgyan Scheme  is available on the Ministry’s website 
(https://ayush.gov.in
).       
In addition to above, Rashtriya Ayu rveda Vidyapeeth (RAV), an autonomous 
body of Ministry of Ayush, conducts a one-y ear certificate course (viz. Certificate of 
Rashtriya Ayurveda Vidyapeeth-CRAV) in Ayurveda, through Guru Shishya 
Parampara, for the purpose of providing addi tional hands on training to the Ayurveda 
doctors having successfully completed the BAMS degree course.  During last three 
years (i.e. 2019-20 to 2021- 22), 491 Shishyas (Ayurveda Doctors) were trained 
under the above course by 196 Gurus. 
 
(c) and (d)  Under Capacity Building & Continuing Medical Education component 
of Ayurgyan Scheme, there is a provisio n for training of teachers, doctors, 
paramedical and other personnel.  Under CME component, financial assistance is 
supported to the eligible institutes/ organizations throughout the Country for 
[ 5 Decemb er, 2023 ]

non-performing assets (NPAs) of schedule d commercial banks (SCBs) for the last 
two financial years are at Annex.  
 Over the last two financial years, the number of NPA accounts in SCBs has 
declined from 2.19 crore to 2.06 crore,  showing a decrease of  6.2%. Similarly, 
aggregate outstanding of such accounts, i.e. gross NPAs in SCBs have declined form 
Rs. 7.41 lakh crore to 5.72 lakh crore during the same period, showing a decline of 
22.9%. Also, slippage ratio (fresh slippage s of NPAs during the financial year as 
percentage of standard loans and advances at the beginning of the financial year) of 
SCBs has declined from 2.74% (fresh sli ppages of Rs. 2.86 lakh crore) in the

financial year 2021-22 to 1.78%  (fresh slippages of Rs. 2.13 lakh crore) in the 
financial year 2022-23.  
 The action against guarantors in cas e of non-repayment of loans can be 
initiated under the extant legal provisions, such as, the SARFAESI Act, 2002, the 
Insolvency and Bankruptcy Code, 2016, etc.

SHRIMATI NIRMALA SITHARAMAN:  Sir, that would take me back… 
 
MR. CHAIRMAN: If you go in a little terms, it is okay.  
 
SHRIMATI NIRMALA SITHARAMAN : Sir, thank you for giving me this opportunity.   
‘Phone banking’ was that time when in Indian banking the people would call the 
banks and say, ‘So and so would come to s eek a loan from your  bank. Please grant 
it.’, meaning, you don’t have to look at his eligibility or his credit ratio.  You will have 
to give him.  So, actually, Sir, today when this debate is being really hot enough to 
talk about  Gross NPA, IMF standard, measuring NPA verus the  Gross Profit  or the 
total loans issued and so on, the heart of the problem was  when during the 10 years 
62
[RAJYA SABHA]

 
of the  UPA’s ruling, between 2004 and 2014, phones were used to ask for delivery of 
loans to customers  who were not worthy of being given a loan.  The burden fell on us 
to sort the Indian banks out with reforms identi fying first of all the four R’s that we talk 
about.   Prime Minister Modiji sat with all of us; my predecessor spent a lot of time; 
Shri Arun Jaitley spent a lot of time understanding where the problem was and 
working together with the Reserve Bank of  India. So, the ‘phone banking’ was the 
method through which the political interferen ce spoilt all our banks and drove them to 
loss making situation. Today, if enthusia sm is shown on asking questions about 
NPAs, first of all, look at   the problem which contributed to NPAs and made Indian 
banks actually having a twin balance shee t problem, and the twin balance sheet 
problem made the Indian economy come down to being a fragile economy.That 
fragile economy today is the fastest growin g and a rate of 7.6 per cent was there in 
the last quarter.   So, kindly do recogniz e these things before questions on NPAs are 
asked.

Steps taken to achieve clean energy target by 2030 
 
*19. SHRI  MUKUL BALKRISHNA WASNIK: Will the Minister of NEW AND 
RENEWABLE ENERGY be pleased to state: 
 
(a) whether it is a fact that India is fa lling behind on delivering the targets of  
increasing its non-fossil energy capacity for achieving the clean energy target 
by 2030; 
(b) the details of the production of non-fossil fuel energy, State-wise; and 
64
[RAJYA SABHA]

 
(c) the steps taken by Government to ensu re production of fifty per cent of the 
country’s energy requirements through renewable energy by 2030? 
 
THE MINISTER OF NEW AND RENEWA BLE ENERGY AND POWER (SHRI RAJ 
KUMAR SINGH): (a) to (c) A Statement is laid on the Table of the House. 
 
Statement 
 
(a) No Sir. India is on track for achi eving its target of having 50 percent of its installed 
source generation capacity from non-fossil fuel  sources by 2030.  So far, a total of 
186.46 GW capacity from non-fossil fuel-bas ed energy resources has been installed 
in the country as on 31.10.2023, which in cludes 178.98 GW Renewable Energy and 
7.48 GW Nuclear Power. In addition, 114. 08 GW of capacity is under implementation 
and 55.13 GW of capacity is under tender ing. The Non-Fossil electricity Capacity 
increased from 32.54% in March, 2014 to 43.82% in Oct, 2023. 
 
(b) Statewise Installed Capacity of non-fo ssil fuel-based energy resources in the 
Country is given at Annexure. 
 
(c) The Government has taken several measur es to promote renewable energy in the 
country, including, inter-alia, the following: 
 
 Permitting Foreign Direct Investment (FDI) up to 100 percent under the automatic 
route,  
 Waiver of Inter State Transmission System  (ISTS) charges for inter-state sale of 
solar and wind power for projects to be commissioned by 30th June 2025,  
 Specifying the minimum share of consumption of non-fossil resources by 
designated consumers, including Distribution Companies, up to 2029-30, 
 Setting up of Ultra Mega Renewable Energy Parks to provide land and 
transmission to RE developers for installation of RE projects at large scale,  
 Schemes such as Pradhan Mantri Kisan Ur ja Suraksha evam Utthaan Mahabhiyan 
(PM-KUSUM), Solar Rooftop Phase II, 12000 MW CPSU Scheme Phase II, 
 Laying of new transmission lines and cr eating new sub-station capacity under the 
Green Energy Corridor Scheme for evacuation of renewable power, 
 Notification of standards for deployment of solar photovoltaic system/devices,  
 Standard Bidding Guidelines for tariff  based competitive bidding process for 
procurement of Power from Grid Connected Solar PV and Wind Projects.  
[ 5 Decemb er, 2023 ]
65

 
 Government has issued orders that powe r shall be dispatched against Letter of 
Credit (LC) or advance payment to ensu re timely payment by distribution 
licensees to RE generators. 
 Notification of Promoting Renewable Energy through Green Energy Open Access 
Rules 2022. 
 Notification of The Electricity (Late Payment Surcharge and related matters) Rules 
(LPS rules). 
 Launch of Green Term Ahead Market (GTA M) to facilitate sale of Renewable 
Energy Power through exchanges. 
 National Green Hydrogen Mission launched with an aim to make India a global hub 
for production, utilization and export of Green Hydrogen and its derivatives. 
 Notification of prescribed trajectory for RE power bids of 50 GW/ annum to be 
issued by Renewable Energy Implementati on Agencies from FY 2023-24 to FY 
2027-28.

[ 5 Decemb er, 2023 ]

SHRI RAJ KUMAR SINGH : Sir, what the hon. Member read out was the established 
solar capacity, not the potential.  The po tential capacity is much more.  What is 
happening is that we set up capacity thro ugh open bids and the preference is for 
areas where the solar insolation is the highest or the wind speed is the highest.   Solar 
insolation is not the highest in Odisha.  It is much higher in Rajasthan, Andhra 
Pradesh, etc.  So, the preference is give n to these areas by the developers.  Since 
the whole country is connected by one grid, it makes sense to harness solar potential 
in those areas first where the insolation is the highest so that the cost of power is less.  
As the hon. Member himself noted, we ar e doing very well in this area.  We have 
pledged that by 2030 we will have 40 per cent of our cap acity from non-fossil fuel-
based sources.  We achieved that in 2021, which is nine years in advance of the target 
date. Today, our established capacity is about 1,86,000 MW, which is about 43 per 
cent of our total established capacity.  The targ et of having 50 per cent of its installed 
source generation capacity from non-fossil fuel sources will be achieved by 2030.  But 
we hope to achieve it before that because , right now, apart from 186 GW, which has 
been installed and I have another 114 GW, which is under implementation, if you add 
all this, it comes to 300 GW.  By 2030, my total installed capacity would be in the 
region of about 780 GW.  Fifty per cent of that is in the region of about, say, 390 GW.  
So, I just have to add about 90 GW more.  We will achieve this target well in advance.  
  
DR. AMAR PATNAIK : If the potential is at a low level, then we will be achieving that 
target. Whatever figures have been mentioned regarding wind energy, solar energy or 
hydropower energy, if the potential is higher, then we should target to achieve that, in 
which case we will have much better potential to achieve a higher growth rate.

explain how we can achieve this target.   
 
SHRI RAJ KUMAR SINGH : Our ambition is not limited to  achieving 50 per cent of our 
capacity.  In fact, our ambition is much higher. As the hon. Prime Minister has 
68
[RAJYA SABHA]

 
announced, by 2030, we will have 500 GWs of established capacity coming from non-
fossil fuel sources.  So, our ambition is actually much higher.   
Now, we already have 300 GWs established.  I need to establish about 200 
GWs to get to 500 GWs.  So, we have laid  down a trajectory of bidding out 50,000 
MWs every year.  That will actually take us  there even before, as we have about 
another six years left till 2030.  If I add 50,000 MWs every year, that will take me to 600 
GWs by 2030.  So, I will be ahead of that target as well.  Now, the problem, which my

MR. CHAIRMAN: Shri P. Chidambaram. Ten minutes, please. 
 
SHRI P. CHIDAMBARAM (Tamil Nadu): Thank you, Sir, more than enough.  Sir, after 
listening to the learned professor of Lucknow University, I am sure that all of us are 
convinced that Amrit Kaal has begun and going by the number of times, he was 
applauded by the Treasury Benches, I think, he should be in line for a ministership 
now. I wish him well.  If all is well in the world and God is in heaven  and India is 
growing at the fastest rate in the world, why is the impact of that growth not felt on 
the ground?  This is the question my friend, Derek asked and I shall try to supplement 
that in the few minutes that I have.  We are a large economy.  We are growing at the 
fastest rate in the world.  We are innovati ve. We have the largest FDI coming into the 
country. But why is it not seen  on the ground? I specifically want to ask, why it is not 
seen in the inflation numbers and the unemp loyment numbers. Let us take only those 
two. Now what are the unemployment number s? I am going by official numbers. The 
Government does not like the CMIE numbers,  so we will avoid the CMIE numbers.  
Mercifully, the BJP no longer talks about two crore jobs being created every year.  
Thank you for small mercies, that you are not making this tall claim which is one of the 
election jumlas. I did not know the word ' jumla' until I heard it first from the hon. 
Home Minister. Now let us focus on a few numbers.  This is official number of the 
periodic labour survey.  The worker popula tion ratio in this country is 46 per cent, 
which means the number of workers to the popu lation is 46 percent. It is quite low, 
but let us take it as acceptable given the various circumstances in the country.  Of the 
worker population, that is, 46 per cent of the total population, male worker population 
is 69 per cent; for the female it is only 22 per cent.  Of the worker population, less 
than 50 per cent actually work.  So, if you apply 50 per cent on 46 per cent, 
effectively, it means only that  23 per cent of the people work.  This has more or less 
been constant in the last nine-and-a-half years of the BJP Government.  Now, why, I 
ask when we have such scintillating grow th, why is this number not improving? My 
learned friend, Derek O'Brien gave the numbers .  In the age group from 15 to 24, the 
unemployment rate is 23.22 per cent.  Amon g the graduates under the age of 25, the 
unemployment rate is 42 per cent. Graduates between the years 25 and 29, it is still 
23 per cent. Graduates between the years of 30 an d 34, it is close to 10 per cent, it is 
9.8 per cent. I spoke at a college, I said, "I am not frightening you, you will graduate 
here, but, unfortunately, one out of the four will be unemployed."And, even  until the  
[ 5 Decemb er, 2023 ]
81

 
age of 35, one out of ten will be unemployed!   Why is this spirited and scintillating 
growth not turning into jobs?  This is the first question I am asking.  Keep in mind, 
most employment in this country is self-emp loyment; it is 57 per cent.  Regular wage 
employees have declined from 24 per cent to  21 per cent!  Apart from the quantity, 
the quality of employment is also poor. So, I want the Government to answer why this 
ten-year splendid period is not reflecting in employment figures.  
 The second one is about price rise.  Ye s; inflation has come down in the last 
couple of months.  I am not disputing that .  But, please remember that even today 
the CPI inflation is above the tolerance limit of 4-6 per cent of the RBI.  Fuel inflation is 
4.3 per cent.  Food inflation is 9.2 per cent. What does the combination of high 
unemployment and high prices mean?   It means – I think, Mr. Derek hinted at that – 
that there is a cut. The impact will be felt  through cuts in domestic consumption, cut 
in household consumption.  The impact is: You borrow more.  Credit growth is driven 
in this country, according to the RBI, on  personal loans and gold loans. The net 
financial assets of households have plumme ted to the lowest level of 5.1 per cent!  
What does it mean?  It means that th e people are consumin g less, people are 
borrowing more, they are liquidating their household assets and savings and their net 
financial savings have come down to a histori cal low, a fifty year low, of 5.1 per cent!  
It also shows up elsewhere. It shows up, for example, in matters like child 
malnutrition. I will just give you the num bers of child malnutrition and they are 
shocking.  Today, among children, you will fi nd that 16.3 per cen t is undernourished, 
19.3 per cent is wasted, 35.5 per cent is stunted!  It show s up again in the food that 
children get.  And, add to Mr. Derek’s nu mbers, under the age of five, so many 
children die due to hunger.  Why is it not sh owing up in consumption?  It also shows 
up in education and mental growth.  Today, in Class VIII, only 70 per cent of children 
can read a Class II text!  And, Sir, on ly 45 per cent of children can do basic 
arithmetic!  In Class VIII, you cannot do basic arithmetic, you cannot read a Class II 
textbook!  So, ultimately, the question is: Wh o is this Government for?  Is it for the 
poor or is its policy so skewed that it favours the rich?  The numbers are undisputed.  
I have not seen a single Government’s spokesman disputing these numbers. Sir, 23 
crore people of India are poor when the pove rty bar is very low.  Poverty bar is Rs. 
1,286 per month per person in urban areas  and Rs. 1,089 per month per person in 
rural areas.  With such a low poverty bar, 23 crore people are poor.  And, it shows up 
an inequality.  The bottom 50 per cent owns 3 per cent of national wealth and gets 13 
per cent of the national income; the top 5 per cent owns 60 per cent of national 
wealth; and, top one per cent earns 22 per cen t of the national income.  Ultimately, 
this kind of growth is unacceptable.  We need a more balanced growth.

external debt is Rs. 172 lakh crores.  In dia has witnessed a surge in private debt,

[RAJYA SABHA]

 
Indian rupee against the US dollar. Due to the extreme rupee-dollar rate fluctuations,

of its youth population, if the Government can’t provide affordable healthcare to its 
poor citizens, there is no point in boasting that our co untry  is the fastest growing 
economy in the world.

SHRI SUJEET KUMAR (Odisha) : Thank you, Mr. Deputy Chairman, Sir.  At the 
outset, I would like to congratulate our countrymen because we have recently 
reached the four trillion dollar economy benchmar k for the first time.  It is well known 
that we have become the third largest economy by purchasing power parity index and 
the fifth largest economy by market exchange rate.  We have amply demonstrated to 
the world our inherent strength, facing th e twin challenges of the Russia-Ukraine 
conflict and also the Covid challenge.  Sir, we are all set to achieve between the 
growth rate of 6.2 to 6.5 or 6.8 per cent.  We can debate on the numbers, but there 
are going to be very healthy growth numbers in the financ ial year 2023-24.  We are 
going to be the fastest growing economy.  Many speakers have already spoken about 
88
[RAJYA SABHA]

 
it.  And, Sir, we are also very likely to become a five trillion dollar economy by 2027.  If 
the economic dots are connected well, by th e end of the Amrit Kaal, by the end of 
2047, we will be a developed economy as well.  This is possible if, as I said, we 
connect all the economic dots properly.  But,  in my view, there are three things that 
the Government needs to focus on to achie ve this goal and the vision.  First and 
foremost is fulfilling the aspirations of our citizens, particularly our youth and women.  
I will elaborate on it at length.  The seco nd one is boosting the MSMEs.  Today, the 
hon. Member, Laxmikant Bajpayee ji, during Zero Hour, also spoke about the need to 
protect and support our MSMEs. I will seco nd him.  I cannot agree with him any 
more.  We need to strongly boost our MSMEs and give impetus to job creation and 
entrepreneurship.  And the third thing is th e need to focus on education, innovation 
and R&D.  In her Budget speech, hon. Finance Minister also gave priority to these 
three areas which I have just referred to.  But,  Sir, I do find some lacuna and I will talk 
about it.  Let me go to the issue of eco nomic empowerment of our youth and our 
women.  Hon. Prime Minister spoke of four  castes recently during one of the election 
speeches.  He spoke of youth, women, farm ers and the poor.  Let us come to the 
youth.  We are the most youthful nation in the world.  Two-thirds of our population is 
under 35 years of age; half of our population  is under 25 years of age.  Hon. Member, 
Dr. Ashok Kumar Mittal was speaking about the demographic dividend turning into 
demographic disaster.  That is a reality, Sir.   That is a genuine concern that we have 
today.  We need to take care of our youth job creation unless we want to see a 
demographic disaster.   
Talking about women empowerment, female worker population ratio is only 22 
per cent, which the hon. Member, Mr. Chid ambaram, was shocked about.  I would 
like to refer to a model of women empowerment from my State of Odisha where 17 
lakh women have been economically empowe red over the years, and the self-help 
groups (SHGs) that they were a part of, are now becoming small and medium 
enterprises (SMEs). So, this is a verifiable model of economic empowerment for 
women which can really help us to achieve our economic goals.   
Then, Sir, I come to the point of doubling farmers’ income. The Government 
has spoken about doubling farmers’ income  by 2022, but it has not happened.  In 
Odisha, we have already double d farmers’ income a couple of years ago.  In fact, we 
have received the Krishi Karman award many times by the Government of India which 
showcases the importance and impetus that we have given to the farmers.   
Talking of inclusive growth, there was a wide gap between the national per 
capita income and the States’ per capita income in the year 2020-21.  Today, Sir, that 
gap has been drastically bridged and we are today almost at par with the national per 
[ 5 Decemb er, 2023 ]
89

 
capita income. Sir, I will give you some st atistics.  The development expenditure of 
Odisha has increased more than  twenty times over the last twenty years.  It is more 
than twenty times over two decades.  This is a humongous spend on development 
expenditure which is shown in all our indicators and parameters.   
 Sir, I will come to education, innovation and R&D.  Way back in 60s, the 
Kothari Commission had recommended that we spend six per cent of our GDP on 
education.  We are nowhere close to that.   
 Sir, I will compliment the Government  of the day and particularly hon. 
Education Minister for this year's Budg et.  The B.E. of education is roughly 
Rs.1,13,000 crore which is a 13 per cent increase over the Revised Estimates of 2022-
23.  Definitely, this is the highest increase in  outlay in expenditure over the R.E. of the 
previous year.  But it is still not enough.  It  is only 2.8 per cent of our Union Budget.  
In contrast, in Odisha, Rs.30,000 crore ha s been earmarked for education in this 
year's Budget which is 12.3 pe r cent of the total budget outlay  of the State.  Sir, 12.3 
per cent of the total budget outlay of the State is for education.   I think the Union 
Government can draw inspiration from Odis h a  o n  t h i s .   I  a m  extremely passionate 
about education.  But I was sorry and sad to note that in her Budget Speech, the 
hon. Finance Minister spoke about seven priorities what she calls Saptrishis but 
education was not one of the priorities or Saptrishis.  We all know that the foundation 
of any nation is education.  No nation can progress economically, socially or 
otherwise without focusing on education.  Th is is why I urge again the Government to 
focus on education.   
 Sir, now I will come to PM POSHAN.  Hon. Member Chidambaram ji spoke 
about child malnutrition.  He gave so me numbers: 16 per cent undernourished 
children and 35 per cent stunted children.  These are the numbers.   Again, I am sad 
to note that the B.E. for 2023-24 for PM PO SHAN is nine per cent less than the R.E. 
of 2022-23.  This will definitely reflect in the poor learning outc omes and educational 
outcomes.   
 Sir, now I will come to R&D.  Hon. Prime Minister has often spoken about 
India becoming one trillion dollar digital economy which will be roughly 20 per cent of 
our economy when we become a five trillion dollar economy.  But tell me which nation 
can become a digital economy without focusi ng on R&D.  Today, we spend only 0.7 
per cent of our GDP on R&D.  Comparable  numbers are:  Israel spends 4.9 per 
cent, USA spends 3 per cent, South Kor ea spends 4.5 per cent, China spends 
roughly 2 per cent and we spend only 0.7 per cent.  I compliment the Government 
and I laud them because they brought in the Anusandhan National Research 
Foundation Bill.  The Prime Minister has often spoken about Jai Jawan, Jai Kisan, Jai 
90
[RAJYA SABHA]

 
Vigyan, Jai Anusandhan .  But we really need to focus on anusandhan or research if 
we want to become a developed economy in the next couple of decades at least. 
 Finally, Sir, I come to the focus on MS MEs.  We are growing.  There is no 
doubt about it.  But it is not a job-led growth .  I am afraid that it is becoming a job-
less growth.  That is because our focus on MS MEs is not to the extent what it should 
be.  Today, MSMEs are crying for capital.   They do not have access to capital.  
There are statistics that if the loan amo unt is less than one cr ore rupees, then the 
default is roughly one per cent.  But if the loan amount is more than 100 crore rupees, 
then the default is over 40 per cent.  Sir, who takes loan over 100 crore rupees?  It is 
the large enterprises.  But the MSMEs take loan worth less than one crore rupees and 
their default rate is less than one per cent.  But still we have not done justice to our 
MSMEs by giving them access to capital and by giving them access to technologies. I 
will again urge the Government..(Time-bell rings.).. The MSMEs are the job creators 
of our country.  They create wealth for the nation.  They contribute 30 per cent to our 
GDP.  They roughly create 123 million jobs, which is roughly 12 crore jobs.  Still we are 
neglecting our MSMEs.  If we have to rea lly focus on the economy , then we need to 
focus on the MSMEs.   
 Sir, finally, since my time is over, I will just conclude by saying that India, that 
is, Bharat, is a Union of States.  If we ha ve to grow socially and economically, then, 
we need to take the States along.  This is why my hon. Chief Minister Shri Naveen 
Patnaik has always believed in the philosoph y of cooperative federalism.  For him, 
empowering the 4.5 crore Odiyas socially  and economically has been his highest 
duty. And this is why he has been appealing in various forums, through the Parliament 
and otherwise, that we need support from the Government of India…

growth.  Currently, we have surpassed the market expectations and registered a 7.6 
per cent growth during the second quarter of 2023-24.  Coming to the tax collections,

the tax collections that has become the norm and this has fueled the economic 
growth.  Within 10 years, the direct tax collections have increased by 160 per cent 
from Rs.6.4 lakh crore in 2013-14 to Rs.1 6.6 lakh crore in Financial Year 2022-23.  
This year, six months from April to Septem ber, tax collections increased by 18 per 
cent.  Till now, the GST collections -- dir ect taxes and indirect taxes -- for this 
Financial Year stand at Rs.13.3 lakh crore averaging to Rs.1.66 lakh crore monthly.

our tax compliance and expanding the tax base .  There is no applaud even from the

SHRI ELAMARAM KAREEM (Kerala) :  Sir, the real state of our economy concerning 
around the people will be cl early understood by the latest RBI report which says that 
the household financial assets, including bank deposits, cash and equity investments, 
fell down sharply to 5.1 per cent of the GDP in  the fiscal year ende d in March, 2023.  
On the other hand, the household debt incr eased to 5.8 per cent of the GDP in the 
financial year, 2023, which is the second highest annual increase since 
Independence.  Recent RBI data also reveals that the bank/financial institutions credit 
to institutions/businesses marked a decline while personal loan portfolios have 
increased in volume during the current quarter which clearly signals further slowing 
down of the economy along with the rise  in peoples' indebtedness and resultant 
distress.  Sir, in addition to these techni cal issues, there are broader concerns about 
the overall economic direction of the count ry.  Tax concessions and incentives are 
given to the rich and corporates and are widening the gap between the two Indias: - 
the rich and the poor.  Real income of the top one per cent, the cronies of Modi 
Government, rose by 30 per cent from Rs. 7. 9 lakh crores in 2019 to Rs.10.2 lakh 
crores in 2022, while real in come of the lowest 25 per cent, who are shouting slogans 
for you, by the 11 per cent from Rs. 3.8 la kh crores to Rs. 3.4 lakh crores.  A 
relentless propaganda continues to boost the Indian economy  resurgence.  The 
recent G20 Summit was used to project Indi a as an emerging economic powerhouse

G20 countries.  That was hidden.  It ranks the lowest in terms of Human Development 
Indices and records the lowest Labour Fo rce Participation Rate,  Mr. Chidambaram 
told here, reflecting the very high level of  unemployment.  The continued decline in 
people's purchasing power is impeding th e economic growth as this is leading to 
declining levels of domestic demand.  This, in turn, leads to declining levels of 
investments.  This means potentially fu rther declining levels of employment 
guarantee.  New proposals for investment in  the economy have rapidly declined over 
the last year - 72.5 per cent in Government sector and 79 per cent in private 
investments.  Massive propaganda is laun ched over the grand success of 'Make in 
India'.  That was the slogan from 2014.  Its target for manufact uring sector growth 
over this decade was 12-14 per cent.  The reality is that since 2013-14, this averaged 
only 5.9 per cent per annum; it was a tall claim; but see where it has gone!  The share 
of the manufacturing to GDP was targeted  to reach 25 per cent.  This, however, 
remains stagnant at around 16.4 per cent.  But, during 2011-12 and 2021-22, jobs in

SHRI VAIKO (Tamil Nadu):  Mr. Vice-Chairman, Sir, I am so thankful to you for giving 
me this opportunity to participate in the discussion on the economic situation in the 
country.  
 India’s current GDP is 3.75 trillion US dollars, which the present BJP 
Government claims that it would go to 5 trillion dollars in 2025.  But, who will be 
benefitted by this growth?  It is a jobless growth.  Unemployment is increasing.  As 
on October, 2023, it is 10.05 per cent.   The educated unemployed youth in the 
country are struggling to get employment opportunities.   
 
[THE VICE-CHAIRPERSON (SHRIMATI DARSHANA SINGH) in the Chair.] 
 
We are witnessing every year one crore jo b loss in our country and the wages of the 
workers are falling drastically.  We are witnessing the continuing agrarian crisis. You 
had promised doubling the income of farmer s.  Unfortunately, farmers’ income did 
not get double, rather, at the same time, su icides of the farmers have got doubled, 
sadly. Our banks are in deep crisis.  Wh at is the remedy that they are putting 
forward? The bank NPAs are rising an d the Government is providing further 
[ 5 Decemb er, 2023 ]
119

 
concessions to the corporates by writing off their loans.  But, they are not prepared to 
reduce or write off the loans of farmers, students, and small entrepreneurs in the 
MSME sector.   
 This country has also faced inflationa ry pressures over the years, influencing 
the current unemployment rate in India.  High inflation rates can erode the purchasing 
power of consumers, leading to reduced demand for goods and services.  This can 
have a cascading effect on business, result ing in cost-cutting measures, including 
layoffs and hiring and firing jobs, leading to higher unemployment rates.  Already there 
is a declined growth rate of 4.3 per cent agai nst 15.5 per cent year over year in trade, 
transport, hotel and communication industry.   
 As I said, inflationary pressures lead to  price rise in the essential commodities, 
raw cotton prices, increase in the inputs fo r agriculture and livestock.  Higher GST 
rate for the common items are reducing th e purchasing power of the common man.  
The structure of public sector undertakings in  your regime is crumbling.  One by one, 
the public sector units are being sold to the corporate giants.

produce 2,500 kg of yarn per day, which is incurring a loss of Rs.1,00,000/- per day.

Even yesterday, in Chennai and neighbouring districts of Chennai, because of the 
cyclone-triggered rain, water bodies have been overflowing and surplus water release 
has caused damage to the infrastructure of Chennai and nearby districts.  The Army, 
NDRF and State agencies are involved in the relief and re sume operations.  But the 
damage caused to the Chennai city and the neighbouring districts need more 
attention immediately.  I request the Centra l Government to take care of the need of 
the people of Chennai and the people living in neighbouring districts at the time of this 
unfortunate disaster which they are not able  to bear because of the velocity of the 
rain.  I request the State Government also to work on a war-footing to bring life to 
normalcy in the districts and the city which have been fully destroyed by the 
continuous rain for the past 24 hours.  To conclude, delta farmers in Tamil Nadu have 
been severely affected due to crop loss.  Their crops have be en marooned because

the farmers to adequately compensate th em for their loss.  I am sure, Madam,

women who manage our households.  Fina lly, farmers are the backbone of our 
country.  I am sure the Government will look  at the farmers in a very lenient way and 
help them.  Thank you.

SPECIAL MENTIONS 
 
Need for Establishment of Fodder Corporation of India to fight stubble burning 
 
SHRI GULAM ALI (Nominated) :  Establishment of a Fodder Corporation of India, 
modelled after the successful Food Corporatio n of India, is imperative to combat the 
escalating menace of environm ental degradation, the ad verse effects of stubble 
burning and scarcity of fodder. The agricultural and animal husbandry sectors, crucial 
components of India’s economy, face a pr essing challenge with the traditional 
practice of burning stubble/crop residues, s carcity of fodder, high prices and animal 
dying due to hunger, contributing significantl y to air pollution and animal devoid of 
adequate fodder.  A dedicated Fodder Corp oration would play a pivotal role in 
addressing this issue by promoting sust ainable agricultural practices. It could 
incentivize farmers to adopt alternative methods for managing crop residues, such as 
converting them into fodder for livestock. By establishing efficient collection centres 
[ 5 Decemb er, 2023 ]
127

 
and processing units, the Corporation can st reamline the conversion of crop residues 
into nutrient-rich fodder, reducing the reliance on environmentally harmful practices 
like stubble burning. 
 Moreover, the Fodder Corporation can act  as a catalyst for rural development 
by creating employment opportunities and promoting the use of eco-friendly 
technologies. By integrating modern farmin g techniques and providing training to 
farmers, the Corporation can enhance agricu ltural productivity while mitigating the 
environmental impact of traditional practic es.  In essence, the establishment of a 
Fodder corporation of India is not just a necessity but a strategic imperative for 
fostering sustainable agriculture, preserving the environment, and safeguarding the 
well-being of future generati ons. It represents a forw ard-thinking approach to 
balance agricultural needs with environmen tal conservation, ensuring a harmonious 
coexistence between farming practices and ecological integrity.   
 I urge the Government to estab lish Fodder Corporation of India.

Demand for Mega Food Storage Plan 
 
SHRI SUBHAS CHANDRA BOSE PILLI (Andhra Pradesh) :  Sir, the World's Largest 
Grain Storage Plan in cooperative sector seeks to address crucial objectives of 
128
[RAJYA SABHA]

 
strengthening market infrastructure and ensu ring better prices for farmers.  However, 
the scheme has faced conflicts with Farm er Producer Organizations (FPOs) and 
inefficiencies in agriculture cooperatives .  India's track record in maintaining 
infrastructure compounds the issue, and th e storage capacity for perishable produce 
remains insufficient.  Managing food qua lity has been problematic as the PDS have 
been known to distribute poor quality grains  due to outdated storage infrastructure.  
There has been a lack of proactive measur es and investment in addressing post-
harvest losses, causing farmers to bear the brunt of these issues.   
 Post-harvest losses in India are due to  insufficient infrastructure, including 
suboptimal storage capacity.  Inadequate  handling and packaging, along with 
deficient transportation infrastructure lead to physical injury and contamination.  Price 
fluctuations increase losses for farmers,  while insufficient pest and disease 
management practices further spoil crops.  Financial constraints, coupled with limited 
access to credit, hinder investment in impr oved facilities and technologies.  I would 
request the hon. Minister of Food and Pub lic Distribution to modernize the existing 
storage, include a separate capacity fo r horticultural crops and introduce more 
schemes focusing on processing perishable items.  Thank you.

Issue of high vacancies in Central Universities 
 
SHRI TIRUCHI SIVA (Tamil Nadu) : Sir, higher educational institutions significantly 
contribute to shaping young talent and the future of the country.  But for years many 
administrative and teaching positions ha ve remained vacant in various Central 
universities. This causes reduced student enrolment, issues on infrastructure 
development, research funding and student services, increased workload on existing 
faculty and restricted research output and innovation.   
 Over 6,500+ faculty positions are vacant across the country - Delhi University 
(900), Allahabad University (622), Bana ras Hindu University (532) and Aligarh 
Muslim University (498) and JNU (326), Jamia Millia Islamia (223), Tripura University 
(146), Central University of Kashmir (116 ), Central University of Haryana (120), 
Hemwati Nandan Bahuguna Garhwal University  (236) as per Ministry of Education, 
2022.   
 The condition is dire especially for the Un iversity of Delhi, one of India's premier 
institutions.  Delhi University and its colleges started selection for permanent positions 
after a gap of 10 years only last September.   
 Faculty shortages can be solved by en gaging research scholars and visiting 
faculty but contract/ ad hoc employment are a short-term solution.  Transparency in 
the selection committee mechanisms an d Academic Performance Indicator 
calculations with equal emphasis on intervi ews and experience is integral.  Education 
outlay was hiked by 8 per cent in the Un ion Budget 2023-24, this needs further 
improvement.  For a future perspective,  pedagogic reforms and synchronization of 
recruitment processes with best global prac tices are needed.  I urge the Government 
to look into the matter.  Thank you very much, Sir.

vulnerable to drought and hilly areas are at risk from land slides.  The Coastal areas of 
Tamil Nadu are increasingly vulnerable to  tropical cyclones and the consequent 
recurrent loss of life and property.  The establishment of Multipurpose Cyclone Centre 
is the main objective of the National Cycl one Risk Mitigation Project and it is to 
minimize vulnerability to cyclones and make people and infrastructure disaster resilient 
in harmony with conservation  of the coastal eco-system in  the cyclone hazard prone 
area.  In the first phase, though th e Government had constructed 567 such 
Multipurpose Cyclone Centres in Odisha, Andhra, Gujarat and West Bengal, but not a 
single MCC was constructed in Tamil Nadu, which has been ravaged frequently than 
any other State in the country.  Out of 353 MCC's in the second phase, 112 in Gujarat, 
27 in Kerala, 11 in Karnataka, 13 in Maharash tra, 150 in West Bengal, 40 in Goa are to 
be constructed.  Tamil Nadu was ravaged continuously from Tsunami in 2004 and 
Cyclones Thane, Nilam, Vardah, Ockhi, Gaja, Nivar and Mandous. 
 I urge the Union Government to includ e Tamil Nadu in the "National Cyclone 
Risk Mitigation Project" to establish Mu ltipurpose Cyclone Ce ntre at vulnerable

Demand to boost Destination Wedding Economy in Bharat 
 
SHRI LAHAR SINGH SIROYA (Karnataka):  Sir, I draw attention to the potential and 
creating awareness about destination weddi ngs in India and its impacts on our 
economy.  Recently, hon. Prime Minister Narendra Modi spoke about rich families 
celebrating destination wedding in foreig n locations and urged citizens to choose 
Bharat as their wedding destination. There is a great possibility of creating destination 
wedding economy in Bharat.   
 While weddings are traditionally viewed as personal celebrations, their 
collective impact on the economy is subs tantial. Wedding festivities contribute 
significantly to the thriving service sector  such as banquet halls, hotels, event 
management, catering, floral decorati on, travel services, photography, music 
orchestras etc.Hon. Chairman, Sir, the consumption of these diverse services 
generates numerous job opportunities. 
According to estimates from the Confed eration of All India Traders, around 
5,000 destination weddings are held abroad  each year with an estimated expenditure 
of Rs. 50,000 crore. This trend underscores the need for concerted efforts to retain 
these celebrations within our country. Bharat boasts a plethora of enchanting locales, 
ranging from breath-taking natural landscap es, sacred temple towns to royal palaces 
and secluded islands. These places have the potential to become destinations not 
only for Indian weddings but also for international events. The spiritual aspect and 
civilizational interest of our land will further attract foreign nationals and Indians staying 
abroad. 
I urge the Government to take proactive initiatives such as policy formulation, 
creation of dedicated portal, or launch of an informative campaign, which would raise 
awareness about the possibilities of hostin g destination weddings within our country 
and contributing positively to our economy.

Demand for enhancement of salary and other service benefits to Anganwadi 
Employees 
 
SHRIMATI JEBI MATHER HISHAM (Kerala) : Sir, the Anganwadi workers and helpers

programmes for the adolescent girls and pregnant women, are now in distress. There 
i s  n o  h i k e  i n  t h e i r  s a l a r y  f o r  t h e  p a s t  seven years. The present monthly salary of 
Anganwadi helpers is Rs. 12,000 and that  of Anganwadi workers is Rs. 8,000 in 
Kerala. This should be enhanced to a mi nimum of Rs. 21,000. There should be a 
provision to bring about their salary revi sion along with that of Government 
employees. Even though subscription amou nt is being collected towards Anganwadi 
workers' and helpers' welfare fund, benefits are not enhanced in accordance with the 
amount collected.  
Anganwadi employees are facing various di fficulties in performing their duties 
on a daily basis. They are not given perfor mance grant for the data collection work in 
respect of various programmes and projects implemented at various levels starting 
from the Panchayat level. Since all of them  do not have smartphones, they find it 
practically difficult to upload this data. Every expense including the payment of 
electricity and water bills of  the Anganwadi centres, travel expenses for attending 
official meetings, etc., have to be born e by them, which is burdening them even 
more.  
In this context, I urge the Central Government to appoint a high-level 
Commission to study the factual position and to make necessary recommendations 
for the betterment of Anganwadi helpers and workers.

Concern over fiscal stability of States 
 
DR. JOHN BRITTAS (Kerala):  Sir, States face fiscal debilitation, with fiscal deficit 
now set at 3.5 per cent of GSDP and 0.5 per cent linked to power sector reforms, 
while Centre’s estimate stands at 5.9 per cent.  In 2018-19, Centre raised 62.7 per 
cent of aggregate resources, while Stat es bore 62.4 per cent of aggregate 
expenditure, covering only 44.8 per cent from their own resources. The rest came 
from vertical resources, transfers and contracting debt. 
 There is a notable revenue devolution  imbalance, exemplified by Kerala’s 
declining tax share from 3.875 per cent du ring the Tenth Finance Commission period, 
when Kerala’s share of India’s population , as per benchmark 1971 Census, was 3.89 
per cent, to around 1.925 per cent now when  State’s population share is 2.76 per 
cent.  Besides, use of population figures of 2011 Census, in lieu of 1971 Census, in 
determining horizontal devolution of revenue resources, led to decline in the share of 
Southern States.  Inclusion of Cess and Surcharges in divisible pool, as there is 
significant increase in Centre’s gross tax re venue from 8.16 per cent in 2011-12 to 
28.08 per cent in 2021-22, amounting to Rs . 7.06 lakh crores, thereby reducing 
States’ effective share in Central taxes. 
 Another constriction is the new stipul ation to reckon public account balances 
and borrowings by State entities when fixing States’ net borrowing limits, while off-
budget borrowings of Central entities are neither considered as Government’s debt 
nor reckoned for fixing its borrowing limits. Sir, 34 per cent of Union revenue is 
estimated from borrowings and other liabilities. 
 Hence, I urge the Government to take steps to ensure fiscal stability of States. 
 
SHRI A.A. RAHIM (Kerala): Sir, I associate myself with  the Special Mention made by 
the hon. Member.
